172
                                        APPENDIX A
                               SUBMARINE AND GHOST SHIPS

                                     MARINE ACCIDENTS

                              FINDS OF MILITARY MATERIAL


Before proceeding to examine the individual cases of sightings collected, it is necessary to say a
few words on the phenomenon of the so-called submarines and ghost ships and the strange
accidents that occasionally occur at sea.

Ghost submarines have been sighted ever since man created this beautiful war machine. The
Second World War, perhaps the most prosperous era for submarines and undoubtedly the period in
which the highest number of boats sailed the seas, is full of observations and false alarms. But
reconstructing such reports in wartime is certainly impossible. The observations of ghost
submarines that most interest this catalog are those that occurred during the Cold War, during that
game of spies, those silent battles that were fought with the sound of sonar and electronic
instruments in the seas all over the world. The phobia of being observed and spied on by the
enemies of the Soviet bloc pervaded almost everyone who went to sea. Certainly the most famous
sighting of a ghost submarine occurred in Norway, in the Sogne fjord in November 1972; on that
occasion an unknown underwater vessel kept around thirty units of the Norwegian fleet in check
for a few days, until it managed to disappear as quickly as it had appeared. Obviously a thousand
conjectures have been made but the main defendants were the Soviets and the Americans.

Even Italy is not immune from ghost submarines and this is easily explainable given that our
country is entirely surrounded by water and is a sort of large "natural aircraft carrier".
It is not known how many of these observations are real and it is almost impossible to make even a
risky estimate. What is certain is that there are still very often observations of large objects moving
under the sea and the phenomenon is certainly interesting and curious. And it is equally certain that
this great spy game has certainly affected our seas.

The interesting numerical fact is that from the 1990s onwards there have never been any more
reports of ghost submarines. This, without any doubt, is due to the end of the world division into
two large blocs, the western one linked to NATO and the eastern one linked to the Warsaw Pact.
With the fall of the Berlin Wall and the collapse of the Soviet empire, it seems that submarines have
stopped "chasing" each other in the Mediterranean. And on the contrary, it is interesting to note that
the greatest number of observations of ghost submarines occurred during the 1970s, i.e. at the
height of the Cold War.

An entire essay could be written about ghost ships. All sailors have always heard of ghost ships,
mythical galleons and unmanned vessels that sail the oceans undisturbed. These stories have a very
particular charm for those who love the sea, even if there is very little that is real.
What I have tried to reconstruct, regarding our country, are all those reports, those false SOS
signals sent by boats in difficulty and never found again by the rescue teams. Of course, some
messages for help are real and perhaps some vessel has really disappeared into the waves, taking its
173
load with it, even if it seems strange that the crew is never spoken about or that everyone quickly
forgets about the unfortunate castaways; but the majority of these reports are well-orchestrated
fakes carried out by unscrupulous smugglers, who in this way have free access to the Italian coasts,
both for cigarette trafficking and for the human trafficking of illegal immigrants.

Perhaps much more interesting is the phenomenon of strange marine accidents, such as those that
happen to fishing boats which, very often, become entangled with their nets in large submerged
objects, which are almost always recognized as submarines.
The reality of these reports (although almost never confirmed with on-site investigations but only
with journalistic articles) is the fact that some boats actually lose their nets, finding themselves
forced to cut the steel cables that secure them. There are countless explanations, from the
submarine to the large cetacean, up to the rock or submerged wreck in which the nets remain
entangled (perhaps the most plausible explanation). An certainly curious fact is that before the fall
of the Soviet empire, fishermen pointed the finger at submarines of Russian or Libyan nationality.
Since the collapse of communism, fishermen have returned to almost exclusively blaming the
Italian Navy and, on some occasions, the US Navy.

Finally, regarding the findings of military material, it must be clarified that anything can be found
in the sea. There is also a saying that everything the sea takes it gives back.
The Mediterranean, especially during the Cold War, was the scene of numerous exercises and it is
therefore highly probable that there are artillery pieces, buoys or torpedoes almost everywhere.
Furthermore, even today the sea gives us some war remnants from the Second World War. The
curious fact, as in the case of accidents between fishing boats and submarines, is that until the end
of the 1980s the material found was almost always of Soviet nationality, with the classic Cyrillic
writings. Again after the fall of the Eastern Bloc, the findings are attributed to the Mediterranean
Navies (Italian, French, Spanish). Furthermore, it is possible to note how the discovery of military
material in the sea occurred after the end of the Second World War and in fact there is not even a
single case before the 1950s.
Also regarding this particular cataloging sector, I must point out that if journalistic sources talk
about sightings of UFOs or aliens, the observation and discovery more properly fall within the
actual UsoCat. In fact, there are some cases in the UsoCat that could be included in Appendix A,
but precisely because the input of the sources were UFOs and not military material, I thought it
correct to include them in the actual UsoCat.

I hope that this very brief introduction has served to make people understand better that the sea is
indeed full of mysteries, but that very often it is man who creates them or does not want to seek a
logical explanation for what happens.

  ??/08/1963       Fregene (RM)          Military material     Investigation by Umberto
                                               found           Cordier of the CISU of
                                                               11/19/2008.
Description of This is the transcript of Cordier's report:
sighting       “Mr. Eugenio of Rome in August 1963, on the beach of Fregene
               (Rome), while lying on the sand, heard (but did NOT see) a loud
               splash                     as                   he                  fell
               into the water; then he would have gotten up and approached towards
               the direction of origin of the noise, finding a point with foamy water
               and then - placing on the seabed - an artificial object which he first
               touched with caution (fearing that it was hot, but it was NOT hot),
               and then drawn from the water; object that he still keeps.
               He didn't see anything else, neither the sky nor the sea and he DOES
               NOT believe it was a UFO event, but possibly a Fortian one. He told
               me                      that                   he                  had
               shown the object to various competent people, but no one was able to
               identify it. So I proposed to him to send me a photo of the object, but
               since        he      didn't       have      one,      he      arranged
               to borrow a digital camera at that moment to then take the photograph
               and send it via email. Examining the photo, we observe a worked
               metal object, with an absolutely ''terrestrial'' appearance and
               attributable to a non-recent mechanical technology, let's say not after
               the Second World War and indeed probably earlier. The object shows
               a set of rods, sights, sliders, engraved curves, graduated scales with
               Arabic numerals and Latin letters; it appears as a pointing and/or
               calculating instrument, a sort of slide rule, and would therefore
178
                 appear to be an object of military, naval or aeronautical use, or a
                 topographical/ballistic instrument.
                 In fact, however, although the object has the appearance of
                 something ''familiar' ', strangely it is not exactly identifiable. Similar
                 objects are not found in the repertoires. For my part, while
                 considering the request for confidentiality, I asked the witness if he
                 would allow me to submit the photograph to an expert I trust. The
                 witness      agreed,     and     so      I    submitted      the    photo
                 to my Ligurian friend Nico Sgarlato (aeronautical journalist), who
                 deals professionally with aeronautics. But even Nico - despite his
                 great             experience               and            documentation
                 - was unable to recognize the object, despite finding some possible
                 affinity with old bomb launcher or torpedo launchers; However, he
                 ruled out that objects of this kind could still be in use in 1963, and
                 probably even during the Second World War. Sgarlato then
                 hypothesized that the object could have already been at the bottom of
                 the sea for some time, when the witness accidentally found it; in this
                 case, however, the very good preservation of the instrument would be
                 surprising. Finally, Sgarlato, in turn, consulted a skilled expert on
                 shipwrecks and marine remnants, but he too said he had never seen
                 anything of the kind, but promised to carry out further research; we
                 are therefore still waiting for this possible response."

Description of One of the most absurd stories that have circulated around the
sighting       tragedy of the explosion of the Itavia DC-9 in Ustica is told to us by
               the testimony of Captain Sergio Bonifacio who was one of the first to
               arrive at the site of the disaster. He himself would have seen,
               according to his statements given to the Public Prosecutor's Office of
               Cagliari, a short distance from the wreckage of the plane, a dark
               black and oblong spot with a black stripe on the back. The Captain,
               an expert Breguet Atlantic anti-submarine aircraft pilot, recognizes
               the object as a submarine . But at this point the incredible would have
               happened, that is, he observed a sudden rise in the water, as if there
               had been a series of underwater explosions on the nacelle of the
               plane, with charges placed by frogmen obviously coming out of that
               submarine, of which the traces would immediately be lost.
               The editor of this catalog does not want to go into the merits of this
               news in the slightest and reports it only for completeness, but it is not
               possible to avoid criticizing all those who use such unconfirmed
               stories in such tragic circumstances and events for our nation. .

               searches would have been carried out by the logistics ship of the
               French Navy "Sabre", with identification L9051. After a few days in
               which newspapers wrote typical Cold War articles, the mystery
               seems to have been resolved with the official declaration that the lost
               torpedo was not French, but an unarmed Italian torpedo, launched
               during experiments by Whitehead Alenia Sistemi Subacquei.

 dd/mm/yy
 07/26/2004        Milazzo (ME)          Military material      “The City of Milazzo” of
                                               found            07/30/2004, page. 16.
Description of The journalistic article by Katia Trifirò is reported verbatim: “ A
sighting       morning to remember for the bathers of Ponente. A few meters from
               the shore, an object of unusual design caused havoc on the stretch of
               beach on Via Aria. That's what it was about. What disturbed the calm
               of a sunny seaside morning on Ponente beach on Monday morning
               (26 July 2004) was a completely unexpected discovery. Carried by
               the waves, after having fallen into the sea who knows how, it
               emerged almost close to the shore, on the stretch of beach on Via
               Aria. The unmistakable elongated shape - about one and a half meters
               - the surface painted in red and white stripes and the small parachute
               that accompanied it left no doubt to the incredulous swimmers: it was
               a missile, of Italian manufacture. The first to notice the unusual
               object was a little girl, who wasted no time in attracting the adults'
               attention. Panic immediately set in. It took just a few minutes for the
               many present to move away from the bomb and settle at a safe
               distance, leaving the field free for the police. After the evacuation of
               the area, the Catania bomb squad intervened and carefully inspected
               the missile, revealing its nature: in reality it was a so-called "towed
               target", used in military exercises. Without explosives and, therefore,
               not as dangerous as had been feared. Once the tension had subsided,
               the bathers were able to breathe a sigh of relief and, already in the
               afternoon, the situation had completely normalised. It is not the first
               time that an explosive device has been accidentally found in Milazzo.
               But, generally, these are remnants of the Second World War,
               unexploded ordnance and so on. Now, however, it remains to be
196
                  clarified how the missile ended up in the water and therefore arrived,
                  dragged by the current, to the waters of the Ponente. Investigations
                  began from Catania, where the missile was immediately transported,
                  which will have to provide an answer to this disturbing question."



    Date              Location                Category                   Sources
 dd/mm/yy
 04/08/2004          Caorle (VE)          Military material      News from the Adnkronos
                                                found            agency of 05/08/2004, 2.02
                                                                 pm; “New Venice” of
                                                                 05/08/2004;             “Il
                                                                 Gazzettino”,        Venice
                                                                 edition of 05/08/2004.
Description of There is a deep mystery in Caorle (Venice) about an aircraft missile
sighting       that was seen sinking by some swimmers (including a soldier) in the
               waters in front of Porto Santa Margherita, at the height of Ponte delle
               Bilance. The object, about ten meters long, disappeared into the sea
               after a long flight at 150 meters above sea level. The editorial offices
               of local newspapers were immediately bombarded with calls, and the
               testimonies speak of a bomb, perhaps dropped by a military plane in
               difficulty that needed to free itself from the uncomfortable load. The
               Port Authority of Caorle, however, has not received any reports. A
               patrol boat equipped with sonar was sent to the site of the supposed
               landing of the missile, without any noteworthy results being
               recorded. The Port Authority itself, in any case, informed the
               command of the Navy in Ancona, to carry out, if not searches, at least
               checks on possible warplanes flying in the area at the time of the
               sighting.
               Here is the verbatim copy of the article that appeared in the
               Gazzettino: “Some swimmers spotted it late yesterday morning in
               front of Porto Santa Margherita. The missile sank into the Adriatic. It
               was ten meters long. It may have been dropped by a plane before
               returning to the Caorle base. A missile was seen flying in the sky of
               Caorle and sinking into the sea in the area in front of Porto Santa
               Margherita. It was some swimmers who spotted him at 11.30
               yesterday. Among them there was a soldier, accustomed to the sight
               of these objects. However, it appears that the missile was off course.
               There is no other way to explain the fact that it ended its race in the
               open sea. "I was on the scale bridge that divides Caorle from the
               hamlet of Porto Santa Margherita - reports the soldier - when I saw a
               missile about ten meters long flying in the direction of the sea, at least
               150 meters high from the ground". Some have speculated that it
               could be one of those missiles that have to be released from planes
               before returning to the ground. The aircraft would otherwise run the
               risk of blowing up. It could be another case of the release of war
               material into the Adriatic Sea as happened during the recent wars,
197
                 when the fishing boats sailing along the Venetian lagoon found the
                 unexploded bombs . The Caorle Harbor Office has received some
                 reports of the sighting of the missile, but not even the shadow of the
                 same. The only traces left on the Caorle body of water are those of
                 two planes, most likely stationed at the USAF base in Aviano, which
                 at the same time were flying at low altitude leaving a trail of white
                 smoke in the sky. The mystery of this missile therefore remains,
                 which has become to all intents and purposes an unidentified flying
                 object. Marco Corazza”.

Description of Here is the article from the Giornale di Sicilia, reported here in full:
sighting       “A satellite probe of the type used to carry out meteorological
               surveys was found yesterday afternoon (17 August) on the seashore
               on the beach of Alcamo Marina. After arriving on site, the
               Carabinieri and Coast Guard attempted to verify the origin of the
               object. The instrument appeared to be in an excellent state of
               preservation, it is white and orange in color and its external casing
               bears two labels indicating that it is an Italian-made object."
